It doesn't really feel like things are moving faster than usual, or like I'm sped up. It's more like... they just wander off track. \n\nThe speed feels completely normal to me. I'll just be going along at my regular pace, trying to get through the shift notes, and the thought just slips away into something else entirely before I even realize it's happening.\n\nDoctor: How long has this problem with your thoughts jumping around been happening? Has it always been like this for as long as you can remember, or did it start at some point more recently?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't always been like this. It definitely started more recently. I'd say... well, the jumbled explanations and those odd stops when I'm out on the sales floor, they've really been noticeable for about six months now.\n\nDoctor: Before these problems started six months ago, did you experience any head injuries, high fevers, infections, or any significant medical events or illnesses?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I haven't hit my head or been sick with a fever or anything. I've been pretty healthy as far as medical stuff goes, no big illnesses or anything out of the ordinary before this all started.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school as a child, did you ever have trouble paying attention in class, or did teachers ever mention that your mind seemed to wander or that you had difficulty staying focused on assignments?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. School was fine for me. I never had any trouble paying attention back then, and my teachers never brought anything like that up. I could focus on my assignments just fine. I mean, I eventually got my bachelor's degree, so studying and focusing wasn't an issue. This whole thing with my thoughts wandering is really just a recent problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any recent accidents, falls, or head injuries in the past six to twelve months, even if they seemed minor at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Nothing like that. No accidents, no falls... definitely haven't hit my head or anything. Like I said, medically, everything has been pretty normal up until this recent stuff with my thoughts.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don't have any trouble physically speaking, the words come out fine. It's just that I'll start trying to explain the inventory numbers, and then my mind just sort of... shifts. Life was just going along normally before all this started.\n\nDoctor: Besides the thought-jumping, have you noticed any involuntary movements, twitching, jerking movements, or repetitive behaviors that you can't seem to control—like facial grimaces, shoulder shrugs, or repetitive gestures?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't noticed any twitching or jerking, like you said. But... I guess I have been doing some weird, repetitive things at work lately. Sometimes I'll catch myself just pacing up and down the same aisle on the sales floor. Or I'll stand there straightening the exact same row of batteries over and over again. And... sometimes I just stand there holding the schedule clipboard, not really doing anything, while the associates are just waiting for me to tell them what to do. I don't really know why I'm doing it, it just happens.\n\nDoctor: When you're pacing that aisle or straightening the batteries over and over, are you aware it's happening while you're doing it, or do you only realize afterward that you've been doing it? And can you stop yourself if you try to, or does it keep happening even when you try to stop?\n\nPatient: I guess... I'm aware of it while it's happening, mostly. Like, I know I'm walking up and down the aisle, or I know I'm touching the batteries again. But it's almost like I'm on autopilot. If I really think about it and tell myself to stop, I can stop. I can walk away. But then a little while later, I might just find myself doing something similar again without really planning to. It's not like I'm being forced to do it, it's just... I just kind of drift into doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any seizures, blackouts, or episodes where you lost consciousness or don't remember what happened?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had anything like that. No seizures or passing out. I remember everything that happens, even when I'm doing those repetitive things... I don't lose time or black out at all.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those repetitive things like pacing the aisle or straightening the batteries, does it feel like you're trying to accomplish something specific, or does it feel more like a purposeless habit or movement that just happens?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm trying to accomplish anything specific. I mean, with the batteries, they're usually already straight. I'm not actually fixing the display or getting work done when I do that. It's more just... it just happens. Like a purposeless habit, I guess. I don't really have a reason for doing it, I just kind of find myself doing it.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these repetitive things like pacing or straightening the batteries, do they always look the same way each time you do them, or do they vary? I mean, pacing is just walking, so I suppose it looks pretty much the same. And touching the batteries... I guess I just run my hand over them the same way. As for how many times a day, I haven't really kept track. It's hard to say... maybe a few times during a shift? I just haven't paid that close attention to the exact number.\n\nDoctor: When you're pacing up and down that aisle, do you follow the exact same path each time—like starting at the same spot, walking to the same end, turning around the same way—or does the path vary?\n\nPatient: I honestly haven't paid that much attention to the exact path. I don't think I start at the exact same spot every time or... or turn around in a specific way. It's really just walking up and down the aisle. \n\nI'm sure it varies a bit, you know, just depending on where I am when I start doing it, or if there's something in the way. I don't lose time or black out at all.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those repetitive things like pacing the aisle or straightening the batteries, does it feel like you're trying to accomplish something specific, or does it feel more like a purposeless habit or movement that just happens?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm trying to accomplish anything specific. I mean, with the batteries, they're usually already straight. I'm not actually fixing the display or getting work done when I do that. It's more just... it just happens. Like a purposeless habit, I guess. I don't really have a reason for doing it, I just kind of find myself doing it.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these repetitive things like pacing or straightening the batteries, do they always look the same way each time you do them, or do they vary? And roughly how many times a day would you say this is happening?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if they look the exact same way every time. I mean, pacing is just walking, so I suppose it looks pretty much the same. And touching the batteries... I guess I just run my hand over them the same way. As for how many times a day, I haven't really kept track. It's hard to say... maybe a few times during a shift? I just haven't paid that close attention to the exact number.\n\nDoctor: When you're pacing up and down that aisle, do you follow the exact same path each time—like starting at the same spot, walking to the same end, turning around the same way—or does the path vary?\n\nPatient: I honestly haven't paid that much attention to the exact path. I don't think I start at the exact same spot every time or... or turn around in a specific way. It's really just walking up and down the aisle. \n\nI'm sure it varies a bit, you know, just depending on where I am when I start doing it, or if there's something in the way. I just haven't noticed any kind of strict pattern to it like that.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these repetitive things like pacing or straightening the batteries, do they happen all throughout your shift in little moments here and there, or do they tend to happen in longer stretches where you're doing the same thing for several minutes at a time?\n\nPatient: I think it's more like little moments here and there. I don't really get stuck doing it for a long time, like several minutes straight. It's just... I'll pace for a bit, or I'll touch the batteries for a few seconds, and then I'll stop or move on to something else. It's just quick things throughout the shift.\n\nDoctor: In the past six months, have you had any episodes of confusion, difficulty thinking clearly, or feeling like your mind was foggy or not working right—separate from the thought-jumping you already described?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had anything like that. My mind doesn't feel foggy or confused... it's just the racing thoughts and the jumping around that I mentioned before. But as far as just not being able to think clearly or being confused, no, I haven't noticed that happening.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes when you're pacing or straightening the batteries, are you fully aware of your surroundings—like noticing your coworkers, hearing conversations, being able to respond if someone talks to you—or does it feel like you're somewhat disconnected or zoned out from what's happening around you?\n\nPatient: I'm fully aware of what's going on. I don't feel zoned out or disconnected at all. If one of my cashiers needs me, or a customer asks a question while I'm doing it, I can stop and answer them right away. I still hear people talking and all the normal store noise... I'm just walking or straightening things, but I'm definitely still present.\n\nDoctor: During or right after these times when you're pacing or straightening the batteries, have you ever experienced any blank spots in your memory—like losing track of time, or having your coworkers tell you that you did something you don't remember doing?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I remember everything I do. I haven't lost any time, and nobody's ever told me I did something I forgot about. My memory is fine during those times."
